clf_binary.predict(X_cc)\n    ordinal_pred = clf_ordinal.predict(X_cc)\n    ordinal_proba = clf_ordinal.predict_proba(X_cc)\n\n    log.info(f\"Binary predictions: human={int((binary_pred == 0).sum())}, AI={int((binary_pred == 1).sum())}\")\n    log.info(f\"Ordinal predictions: {dict(zip(*np.unique(ordinal_pred, return_counts=True)))}\")\n    log.info(f\"Mean AI probability: {binary_proba.mean():.4f}\")\n\n    # ── Step 6: Compute additional vocab richness metrics ──\n    log.info(\"=\" * 60)\n    log.info(\"Step 6: Additional vocabulary richness metrics\")\n    log.info(\"=\" * 60)\n\n    extra_metrics = compute_extra_vocab_metrics(cc_texts)\n    yules_k = np.array([m[\"yules_k\"] for m in extra_metrics])\n    mean_word_len = np.array([m[\"mean_word_len\"] for m in extra_metrics])\n    vocab_growth = np.array([m[\"vocab_growth_rate\"] for m in extra_metrics])\n    log.info(f\"Extra vocab metrics computed for {len(extra_metrics)} texts\")\n\n    # ── Step 7: Correlation analysis ──\n    log.info(\"=\" * 60)\n    log.info(\"Step 7: Correlation analysis\")\n    log.info(\"=\" * 60)\n\n    correlation_targets = {\n        \"ttr\": X_cc[:, FEAT_COLS.index(\"ttr\")],\n        \"hapax_ratio\": X_cc[:, FEAT_COLS.index(\"hapax_ratio\")],\n        \"freq_entropy\": X_cc[:, FEAT_COLS.index(\"freq_entropy\")],\n        \"low_freq_ratio\": X_cc[:, FEAT_COLS.index(\"low_freq_ratio\")],\n        \"self_bleu\": X_cc[:, FEAT_COLS.index(\"self_bleu\")],\n        \"surp_mean\": X_cc[:, FEAT_COLS.index(\"surp_mean\")],\n        \"surp_std\": X_cc[:, FEAT_COLS.index(\"surp_std\")],\n        \"yules_k\": yules_k,\n        \"mean_word_len\": mean_word_len,\n        \"vocab_growth_rate\": vocab_growth,\n    }\n\n    corr_with_ai_prob = {}\n    for name, values in correlation_targets.items():\n        rho, pval = stats.spearmanr(binary_proba, values)\n        corr_with_ai_prob[name] = {\"rho\": round(float(rho), 4), \"pval\": float(pval)}\n        log.info(f\"  AI_prob vs {name}: rho={rho:.4f}, p={pval:.2e}\")\n\n    corr_with_depth = {}\n    for name, values in correlation_targets.items():\n        rho, pval = stats.spearmanr(ordinal_pred, values)\n        corr_with_depth[name] = {\"rho\": round(float(rho), 4), \"pval\": float(pval)}\n        log.info(f\"  pred_depth vs {name}: rho={rho:.4f}, p={pval:.2e}\")\n\n    depth_stratified = {}\n    for d in range(int(ordinal_pred.max()) + 1):\n        mask = ordinal_pred == d\n        if mask.sum() == 0:\n            continue\n        depth_stratified[str(d)] = {\n            \"count\": int(mask.sum()),\n            \"ttr_mean\": round(float(X_cc[mask, FEAT_COLS.index(\"ttr\")].mean()), 4),\n            \"hapax_ratio_mean\": GPU3 is using 3417 MiB, which means the model is loaded. However, the log shows it's stuck in an infinite loop of HuggingFace API tree listing requests for the C4 dataset. The shard URL approach is failing and it's falling back to `load_dataset("allenai/c4", "en", split="validation", streaming=True)`, which needs to list all the dataset files first. This is creating many API requests.

Wait, looking more carefully at the log, it seems like the `load_dataset("json", data_files=shard_url, ...)` with the direct URL failed (because the HF_ENDPOINT is set but the shard URL is being constructed with the HF_ENDPOINT... 

Actually, I see the issue now—the HTTP requests are going to `huggingface.co` instead of `hf-mirror.com`, so the `HF_ENDPOINT` environment variable isn't being respected by the datasets library's API calls. The shard URLs were constructed with `hf-mirror.com` for downloading, but the tree listing API is still hitting the default endpoint, which is causing the bottleneck.

Wait, looking at the responses more carefully—they're actually returning 200 OK, so it's not failing. The C4 dataset just has thousands of files across multiple shards, so the tree listing is taking a long time to complete. This is normal behavior, just slow. I should wait to see if it eventually starts streaming the actual data. I need to stop this process and rewrite the data loading logic to be more efficient—either by using a direct shard URL with the proper endpoint or switching to a cached local dataset instead of paginating through thousands of C4 files.

### Assistant

_source line 320_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
I see the issue now. The `load_dataset("json", data_files=shard_url, ...)` call seems to have triggered the HuggingFace datasets library to do some validation which involved listing the C4 repository tree. The shard URL contains the C4 repo path, and the library is resolving the file via the API, which means it's listing all 1024+ train shards plus validation shards.

Looking at the log, the shard URL approach did trigger a successful request to hf-mirror.com for the revision and first tree page, but then subsequent tree listing requests went to huggingface.co (not hf-mirror.com). 

So this is a known limitation where the datasets library uses HF_ENDPOINT for some requests but falls back to the main huggingface.co domain for paginated tree listings. The requests are all succeeding with 200 OK responses, just taking longer than expected. Let me check if the process has moved past this stage.
```

</details>
